 And so I think

 Now we're also
 required to do
 it under the
 Place for All
 legislation
 that was passed
 last June.
 And to refresh
 your memory,
 colleagues,
 that
 legislation
 made it the
 policy of the
 City
 and County of
 San Francisco
 to provide
 immediate exits
 from the street
 for everyone
 willing
 to accept them.
 And it had
 taken us a long
 time, that is
 me and others
 on the board
 who supported
 this
 idea and the
 folks out in
 the
 neighborhoods
 and the small
 business
 community and
 otherwise
 nearly two
 years to get
 the City to
 make that
 commitment.
 At the time, we
'd hoped that
 the passage of
 this board
 policy would
 begin to shift
 the
 emphasis of the
 City's efforts
 away from
 producing as
 much permanent
 supportive
 housing as
 possible
 to a more
 balanced
 approach that
 would also
 prioritize
 producing
 enough shelter
 and other
 immediate exits
 from the
 streets to
 eliminate or
 meaningfully
 reduce street
 encampments
 in the City.
 This was, I
 believe, a
 significant
 change in
 policy
 direction
 driven by a
 growing
 realization
 among San Franc
iscans and more
 broadly across
 the Bay Area
 that shelter
 for all is a
 humanitarian
 imperative and
 the quickest
 way to end uns
heltered
 homelessness
 with limited
 resources.
 The Bay Area
 Council
 Economic
 Institute's
 2021 report on
 Bay Area
 homelessness
 titled
 New Urgency,
 New Solutions
 found that a
 Bay Area
 shelter mandate
 could shelter
 the vast
 majority of the
 region's
 homeless
 population for
 approximately $
245 million in
 one-time
 capital
 expenditures
 and $481
 million in
 annual spending
 on services and
 management.
 Similarly, All
 Homes 2021
 Regional Action
 Plan states
 that in order
 to reduce uns
heltered
 homelessness
 rapidly, most
 cities or
 counties will
 need to front-
load investment
 into interim
 housing options
 such as leasing
 or purchasing
 motels, tiny
 homes, mobile
 homes, or other
 temporary
 housing options
.
 There's strong
 evidence that
 shelter for all
 policies work.
 In New York
 City where
 residents have
 a right to
 shelter, 96.2%
 of people
 experiencing
 homelessness
 have some form
 of shelter or
 transitional
 housing.
 Here in San
 Francisco, only
 57% of people
 experiencing
 homelessness
 have some form
 of
 shelter.
 HSH issued its
 Place for All
 report on
 December 30,
 2022 and I want
 to thank the
 department
 staff for their
 work on the
 report.
 It includes
 important data
 on racial

 of finding

 has struggled

.
 The report

45 billion plan
 to add nearly 4
,000 more

 years with

, $57 million

172 million.
